  ---
  TripPin OData v4 analysis

  0. The thing that dominates the design: session-scoped URLs

  https://services.odata.org/v4/TripPinServiceRW/ does not serve data. It
  302-redirects to a session URL:

  https://services.odata.org/v4/(S(4ntrm43nvbm3lnydmlt0moju))/TripPinServiceRW/

  Every fresh hit on the bare URL mints a new session with pristine data. I
  verified that a PATCH in session A is invisible in session B. So the app must
  resolve the redirect once at startup, cache that session base, and issue every
  subsequent request against it. If you let each request follow the redirect
  independently, every write silently disappears. This is the single biggest
  constraint on the architecture.

  ---
  1. People entity set

  Entity set People, type Microsoft.OData.SampleService.Models.TripPin.Person,
  declared OpenType="true".

  Key: UserName (Edm.String), single-part. Annotated Core.Permissions/Read, so
  it is read-only. URL form is People('russellwhyte'), quoted string key.

  ┌─────────────┬────────────────────────┬────────────┬─────────────────────┐
  │  Property   │          Type          │  Nullable  │        Notes        │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ UserName    │ Edm.String             │ no         │ Key, read-only      │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ FirstName   │ Edm.String             │ no         │                     │
  │             │                        │ (required) │                     │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ LastName    │ Edm.String             │ no         │                     │
  │             │                        │ (required) │                     │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Emails      │ Collection(Edm.String) │ yes        │                     │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ AddressInfo │ Collection(Location)   │ yes        │ Complex, see below  │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Gender      │ PersonGender enum      │ yes        │ Male=0, Female=1,   │
  │             │                        │            │ Unknown=2           │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │             │                        │            │ Core.Computed,      │
  │ Concurrency │ Edm.Int64              │ no         │ server-managed ETag │
  │             │                        │            │  source             │
  └─────────────┴────────────────────────┴────────────┴─────────────────────┘

  Location is an open complex type: Address (string, non-null) plus City
  (non-null), where City has CountryRegion / Name / Region, all non-null.

  Three caveats the metadata does not tell you, all confirmed by testing:

  - Nullable="false" is not enforced. A PUT omitting LastName returned 204, not
  a validation error. Validate client-side or you will get no server-side safety
  net.
  - OpenType="true" means typos are accepted. I patched
  {"MyCustomField":"hello"} and it persisted with a 204. A misspelled field in
  your update payload will not error, it will just add junk to the entity.
  - Concurrency overflows JavaScript. The value 639210892429244237 is well past
  Number.MAX_SAFE_INTEGER, so JSON.parse corrupts it. Don't use it as your
  concurrency token, use the @odata.etag string (see §4). If you ever genuinely
  need the number, request IEEE754Compatible=true in the Accept header to get
  int64s as strings.

  ---
  2. Navigation properties: scope them out

  ┌───────────┬────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │   Nav     │        Type        │                 Notes                  │
  │ property  │                    │                                        │
  ├───────────┼────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Friends   │ Collection(Person) │ Recursive, bound back to People        │
  ├───────────┼────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Trips     │ Collection(Trip)   │ ContainsTarget="true", contained, only │
  │           │                    │  reachable via People('x')/Trips       │
  ├───────────┼────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Photo     │ Photo              │ Media entity, HasStream="true"         │
  └───────────┴────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Recommendation: scope all three out of v1. None are needed for list, search,
  detail, or update. Concretely:

  - The entity set declares InsertRestrictions with Trips and Friends as
  non-insertable, so you cannot create them through People anyway.
  - Trips being contained means it is a separate resource path with its own
  lifecycle, which is a whole second CRUD surface. That is a feature in its own
  right, not part of "manage a person."
  - Photo is a media stream, needing image/jpeg handling and a completely
  different upload path.

  The one cheap exception worth considering: on the detail page only,
  $expand=Friends($select=UserName,FirstName,LastName) is a single extra round
  trip and gives you a read-only "Friends" list. I confirmed it returns 200.
  Treat it as display-only; do not build friend add/remove.

  ---
  3. Query options, all verified live against People

  ┌──────────────┬────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │    Option    │ Status │                  Notes                   │
  ├──────────────┼────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ $select      │ works  │                                          │
  ├──────────────┼────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ $filter      │ works  │ see functions below                      │
  ├──────────────┼────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ $expand      │ works  │ incl. nested $expand=Trips($select=Name) │
  ├──────────────┼────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ $top / $skip │ works  │                                          │
  ├──────────────┼────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ $orderby     │ works  │ incl. desc                               │
  ├──────────────┼────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ $count=true  │ works  │ returns "@odata.count": 20               │
  ├──────────────┼────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ $search      │ works  │ SearchRestrictions/Searchable=true       │
  └──────────────┴────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘

  $filter expressions I confirmed return 200: contains(), startswith(),
  tolower(), Emails/any(e: contains(e,'contoso')), nested AddressInfo/any(a: 
  a/City/Name eq 'Boise'), and and/or combinations.

  Metadata declares this full FilterFunctions set: contains, endswith, 
  startswith, length, indexof, substring, tolower, toupper, trim, concat, year, 
  month, day, hour, minute, second, round, floor, ceiling, cast, isof.
  Conformance level is Advanced.

  Three practical traps:

  - Server-driven paging is 8 rows. 20 people total, 8 per page, with
  @odata.nextLink carrying a $skiptoken. Your list must either follow nextLink
  or drive its own $top/$skip. Do not assume one request gets everything.
  - Enum filters must be fully qualified. Gender eq 
  Microsoft.OData.SampleService.Models.TripPin.PersonGender'Female' returns 200.
  The natural-looking Gender eq 'Female' returns 500.
  - An unknown field in $filter returns 200 with zero rows. $filter=NoSuchField 
  eq 1 does not error, it returns an empty set. A typo in a search box looks
  exactly like "no results," which is nasty to debug.

  ---
  4. PATCH / PUT and the ETag requirement

  Both are supported and both return 204 No Content. The endpoint is genuinely
  read-write (RW suffix), sandboxed per session.

  ETags are mandatory. Measured:

  ┌─────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────┐
  │           Request           │          Result           │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ PATCH with no If-Match      │ 428 Precondition Required │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ PATCH with If-Match: *      │ 204                       │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ PATCH with stale If-Match   │ 412 Precondition Failed   │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ PATCH with current If-Match │ 204                       │
  └─────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────┘

  So the update flow is necessarily read-then-write: GET the person, keep its
  ETag, send it back in If-Match. Use If-Match: * only where you deliberately
  want last-write-wins.

  Further quirks, all measured:

  - 412 and 428 return completely empty bodies. No OData error JSON. Error
  handling must branch on the status code, because there is nothing to parse.
  - PUT behaves like a merge, not a replace. Omitting LastName and Emails left
  both intact rather than clearing them. Do not use PUT to null out fields; it
  will not do what the spec implies. Given this, just use PATCH throughout,
  there is no behavioral gain from PUT.
  - PATCH on UserName returns 204 and silently does nothing. The key is
  immutable, but you get a success status. Never surface the key as an editable
  field.

  Other service quirks worth designing around

  GET on a nonexistent person returns 204 No Content, not 404. Verified on both
  a used and a completely fresh session. A naive if (res.ok) check passes, then
  you parse an empty body. Your detail route must explicitly treat 204 / empty
  body as not-found.

  CORS is fine for the request, but the ETag header is not readable from the 
  browser. Preflight returns Access-Control-Allow-Methods: GET, POST, DELETE, 
  OPTIONS, PATCH and Access-Control-Allow-Headers: Content-Type, Accept, 
  If-Match, so a browser PATCH with If-Match is allowed. However there is no 
  Access-Control-Expose-Headers, so JS cannot read the ETag response header
  cross-origin. Read the @odata.etag property from the JSON body instead. It
  carries the identical value and is not subject to CORS header filtering. This
  one would otherwise cost real debugging time.

  Do not use @odata.editLink / @odata.id for requests. They come back as
  http://services.odata.org/V4/..., lowercase-to-uppercase path change and plain
  HTTP. From an HTTPS page, using them directly triggers mixed-content
  blocking. Build URLs from your own cached session base.

  ResetDataSource exists and works. POST {base}ResetDataSource returns 204 and
  restores original data. I confirmed a mutated FirstName reverted. Good for
  test teardown, though per-session isolation already gives you most of that for
  free.

  ---
  Suggested scope

  Build against People only: list with server paging, search via $filter on
  contains/startswith over FirstName/LastName/UserName, detail via
  People('key'), and update via PATCH with If-Match sourced from @odata.etag.
  Editable fields: FirstName, LastName, Emails, Gender. Read-only: UserName,
  Concurrency. Defer AddressInfo (nested complex collection, a fiddly form for
  little value), and defer Friends, Trips, Photo entirely.

  The two infrastructure pieces to get right before any feature work: the cached
  session base URL, and a fetch wrapper that treats 204-on-GET as not-found and
  threads ETags through updates.

> Based on the metadata analysis above, propose an architecture for this app:   
  - Onion/Clean Architecture with 4 projects: Domain, Application,              
  Infrastructure, Presentation (console), plus a Tests project per layer where  
  it adds value.                                                                
  - Domain: Person entity/value objects, no external dependencies.              
  - Application: use cases (ListPeople, SearchPeople, GetPersonDetails,         
  UpdatePerson) as interfaces + handlers (simple CQRS-lite, no MediatR          
  dependency required — justify if you do add it).                              
  - Infrastructure: OData client implementation of an IPersonRepository (or     
  IPeopleService) port defined in Application. Use Microsoft.OData.Client or a  
  typed HttpClient + raw OData query strings — recommend one and justify        
  briefly.                                                                      
  - Presentation: console menu loop, thin, only orchestration and I/O.          
  - Cross-cutting: configuration (appsettings.json + IOptions), logging         
  (Microsoft.Extensions.Logging / Serilog), resilience (Polly: retry with       
  jitter + circuit breaker on the OData HttpClient), DI via                     
  Microsoft.Extensions.Hosting generic host.                                    
  Also address, briefly, as design notes (not over-engineering the console app  
  itself):                                                                      
  - Thread-safety: HttpClient reuse via IHttpClientFactory, no shared mutable   
  state across concurrent requests.                                             
  - If I allow concurrent searches/operations (e.g. user triggers overlapping   
  requests, or a batch update path), how would cancellation (CancellationToken) 
  and safe concurrent access to any local cache be handled?                     
  - A simple in-memory cache (IMemoryCache) for GET People with sensible TTL    
  and explicit invalidation on update — explain the tradeoff vs always hitting  
  the API.                                                                      
  Output: a directory tree with one-line responsibility per project/folder, and 
  a short ADR (Architecture Decision Record) in markdown explaining the OData   
  client choice, the caching choice, and why Onion over a flat layout. Don't    
  write implementation code yet.                                                

● Good, the metadata work pays off directly here: three of those findings
  (session URLs, mandatory ETags, 204-not-404) are architectural drivers, not
  implementation details, and they shape the ports rather than hiding inside
  Infrastructure.

  Two corrections to my earlier analysis now that the target is a console app, 
  not a browser: the CORS/Access-Control-Expose-Headers problem is irrelevant
  (no preflight, the ETag header is readable), and the Concurrency Int64
  precision issue disappears since C# has a real Int64. Both caveats were
  browser-specific. The mandatory-If-Match finding is unaffected and still
  drives the design.

  Target: .NET 10 (LTS), Microsoft.Extensions.Hosting generic host.

  ---
  How the metadata findings land in the design

  Session URL is the one piece of genuinely tricky infrastructure.
  SessionUriProvider is a singleton holding a Lazy<Task<Uri>>, so N concurrent
  callers trigger exactly one resolution and all await the same task.
  SessionUriHandler then rewrites each outgoing relative URI onto that base.
  Handler order matters: resilience outermost, session rewriting inner, so a
  retried request re-evaluates the session (and a failed resolution is not
  cached permanently). Resolution itself is a one-shot GET with auto-redirect
  that reads response.RequestMessage.RequestUri.

  ETag flow is explicit and crosses every layer, because it has to. Person
  carries a ConcurrencyToken; GetPersonDetails returns it; UpdatePerson requires
  it. The port therefore exposes GetForUpdateAsync separately from GetAsync:
  the former always bypasses cache. This is the single most important
  interaction between two findings, and §Caching explains why.

  204-not-404 is normalised in ODataStatusInterpreter and never leaks upward.
  Application sees Result.NotFound, not a status code.

  428 should be unreachable by construction, since the only write path always
  sends If-Match. If one ever surfaces, treat it as a bug, not a transient 
  fault: log at error and fail fast rather than retry.

  Server paging (8/page) is hidden behind PagedResult. Drive with $top/$skip
  plus $count=true so the console can show "page 2 of 3", but still tolerate a
  @odata.nextLink if the service returns one.

  Silent-empty filters are why PersonFilter is a structured type and raw OData
  strings never cross the port. A typo becomes a compile error instead of a
  mystery empty result set.

  ---
  Design notes

  Thread-safety

  One HttpClient per logical client via IHttpClientFactory (typed client
  registration), so socket exhaustion and stale DNS are both handled. The
  repository is stateless and registered transient: no fields beyond injected
  singletons. SessionUriProvider is the only shared mutable state in the app,
  and its mutation is confined to a single Lazy<Task<Uri>> assignment. This
  statelessness is not incidental, it is the main reason for the client choice
  in ADR-001.

  Cancellation and concurrent operations

  CancellationToken is a required parameter on every port and handler method,
  threaded unbroken to HttpClient.SendAsync. No CancellationToken.None defaults,
  which otherwise quietly make cancellation unobservable.

  The console composes three sources per action with CreateLinkedTokenSource: a
  process-wide token from Console.CancelKeyPress (with e.Cancel = true for
  graceful shutdown), a per-operation timeout, and a supersede token.
  Overlapping searches use last-one-wins: OperationScope holds the current CTS
  and Interlocked.Exchangees it, cancelling the outgoing one so a slow first
  search cannot overwrite a fast second one's results.

  For a batch update path, bound concurrency with Parallel.ForEachAsync and a
  small MaxDegreeOfParallelism (4 or so), since this is a shared public sandbox.
  Note from the metadata that BatchContinueOnErrorSupported is false, so OData
  $batch aborts the remaining operations on first failure. For partial-success
  semantics, issue individual requests and aggregate results rather than using
  $batch. Also remember each update needs its own fresh ETag, so a batch is
  inherently N reads plus N writes.

  Caching

  IMemoryCache behind a decorator (CachedPeopleRepository), so caching is a
  composition-root concern and the use cases stay oblivious. Suggested TTLs: 60s
  absolute for list/search results, 120s for single-person reads, plus a
  SizeLimit so the cache cannot grow without bound across many distinct queries.

  Two implementation points that are easy to get wrong:

  - GetOrCreate is not atomic. Concurrent misses on the same key invoke the
  factory more than once. Cache a Lazy<Task<T>> (or AsyncLazy<T>) as the value,
  so the first caller creates the task and the rest await it. That gives
  stampede protection without a lock per request.
  - IMemoryCache has no tag eviction. For "invalidate every list result after an
  update", register all list entries against a shared CancellationChangeToken,
  then cancel and replace that token on write. Detail entries are evicted
  directly by key.

  The tradeoff, and the part that actually matters here. Caching buys real
  latency wins on a menu loop where the user pages back and forth over 20
  records, and cuts load on a shared sandbox. The cost is staleness, which is
  normally benign for a list. It is not benign for ETags: serving a cached
  entity as the source of an If-Match value produces a 412 the moment anything
  else has touched that record. Hence GetForUpdateAsync bypasses cache
  unconditionally. A stale list is a cosmetic annoyance; a stale ETag is a
  failed write.

  Given this service is sandboxed per session and effectively single-user,
  caching is a modest win. It earns its place mainly because the decorator makes
  it nearly free to add and trivial to remove, not because the workload demands
  it.

  Resilience

  Polly v8 via Microsoft.Extensions.Http.Resilience: exponential backoff with
  jitter (DelayBackoffType.Exponential, UseJitter = true, 3 retries) plus a
  circuit breaker, both on the typed client.

  Retry only on 5xx, 408, 429 (honouring Retry-After), and network faults.
  Explicitly exclude 412 and 428, and keep them from counting toward the
  breaker. Both are semantic outcomes, not transient faults: retrying a 412
  re-sends the same stale ETag and fails identically, while burning the budget
  and potentially opening the circuit against a perfectly healthy service.

  ---
  ADR

  ADR-001: Typed HttpClient with hand-built OData queries, not 
  Microsoft.OData.Client

  Status: Accepted

  Context. Two viable clients. Microsoft.OData.Client generates a typed proxy
  from $metadata, gives LINQ-to-OData, and tracks ETags automatically via
  DataServiceContext. The alternative is a typed HttpClient with a small query
  builder and manual DTO mapping.

  Decision. Typed HttpClient.

  Rationale.

  1. DataServiceContext is stateful and not thread-safe. It is a unit-of-work
  with an entity tracker, so supporting overlapping searches means a context per
  operation, which discards the change-tracking that is its main selling point.
  A stateless repository is thread-safe by construction.
  2. It does not integrate with IHttpClientFactory. It owns its request
  pipeline, so injecting a Polly handler chain means working through
  Configurations.RequestPipeline and bypassing the factory. Since resilience and
  lifetime management were stated requirements, this alone is close to
  decisive.
  3. Every quirk needs HTTP-layer interception. Session redirects,
  204-as-not-found, and 428 mapping all live naturally in a DelegatingHandler or
  a response interpreter. Through the typed client they are awkward at best.
  4. The codegen never amortises. The app touches one entity set with four
  operations. We would generate the full model (containment, media streams,
  bound functions) to use a sliver of it, then fight it on the parts that
  matter.

  Consequences. We hand-write query strings, which is a real escaping risk.
  Mitigated by confining construction to ODataQueryBuilder, keeping raw OData
  strings out of the port entirely (PersonFilter is structured), and covering
  the builder with golden-string tests, including the fully-qualified enum form
  that a naive Gender eq 'Female' gets a 500 for. We also hand-map DTOs, which
  is mechanical and testable. If this ever grew to cover Trips, Photos, and
  bound functions, the calculus would flip and this decision should be
  revisited.

  ---
  ADR-002: Cache reads behind a decorator; never cache ETags

  Status: Accepted

  Context. A console menu re-reads the same small dataset repeatedly.
  IMemoryCache is cheap. But the service demands If-Match on every write and
  returns 412 for a stale token.

  Decision. Cache reads in an IPeopleRepository decorator with short TTLs (60s
  lists, 120s details) and explicit invalidation on update. Expose
  GetForUpdateAsync as a distinct, never-cached path for the read-before-write.

  Rationale. The decorator keeps IMemoryCache out of Application entirely, so
  caching is a composition-root wiring choice and can be removed by deleting one
  registration. Splitting the read-for-display path from the read-for-update
  path makes the ETag freshness rule structural rather than a comment someone
  has to remember.

  Consequences. Lists can be up to 60s stale, which is acceptable for browsing.
  Updates always cost an extra round trip, which is correct and unavoidable
  given 428. Invalidation needs a CancellationChangeToken generation pattern
  because IMemoryCache lacks tag eviction, and cached values are Lazy<Task<T>>
  to prevent stampedes. Accepted alternative if this proves fiddly: drop list
  caching entirely and keep only the freshness-critical paths, since 20 records
  over a fast API do not really need it.

  ---
  ADR-003: Onion over a flat layout

  Status: Accepted

  Context. A console app against one entity set could plausibly be a single
  project. Four projects plus three test projects is more ceremony than the
  feature count alone justifies.

  Decision. Onion, with dependencies pointing inward only: Console →
  Infrastructure → Application → Domain.

  Rationale. The justification is not size, it is that this service's quirks are
  unusually invasive. Session-scoped URLs, mandatory ETags, 204-as-not-found,
  silent-empty filters, and an open type that swallows typos are all things
  that, in a flat layout, smear across the codebase and get re-solved
  inconsistently. A port boundary forces them to be normalised in exactly one
  place. The concrete payoff: IPeopleRepository lets us test all four use cases
  against a fake, and test quirk handling against a stubbed HttpMessageHandler,
  with no network in either case, which matters when the live service resets
  state per session and is shared with the public.

  It also keeps the Domain honest about something the service is not: the
  metadata marks FirstName and LastName non-nullable but does not enforce it,
  and open types accept arbitrary misspelled properties. A dependency-free
  Domain is where those invariants get enforced for real.

  Consequences. More projects, more wiring, one more indirection to trace when
  reading code. We accept that, but deliberately cap it: no
  repository-per-entity generalisation, no generic IRepository<T>, no MediatR,
  and no test project for the console.

  On MediatR specifically. Not adopted. Four use cases do not need runtime
  dispatch, and the cross-cutting concerns it usually justifies (logging,
  caching, validation) are handled here by DI decorators, which are
  compile-time-checked and easier to follow than a behaviour pipeline. Two
  hand-rolled interfaces cost roughly twenty lines. There is also a licensing
  consideration: recent MediatR versions moved to a commercial license for
  larger organisations, which is worth confirming against your situation before
  taking the dependency anywhere. If the use case count grows past roughly
  fifteen, or genuinely order-sensitive pipeline behaviours appear, revisit it.

  ---
  Two calls I made that are worth confirming before I scaffold: the console gets
  no test project, and AddressInfo is read-only in v1 (displayed on the detail
  screen, absent from the edit screen), since a nested complex collection is a
  disproportionate amount of form-handling for a console UI. Say the word if you
  want either changed.

  Want me to write the ADRs to docs/adr/ and scaffold the solution and project
  files next?

● Answered definitively, and the answer changes two small things in the design.

  Scalars are not the only meaningful update surface

  Field: FirstName / LastName
  Kind: scalar string
  Writable?: yes
  What it exercises: baseline
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Field: Emails
  Kind: Collection(Edm.String)
  Writable?: yes
  What it exercises: collection replace semantics, empty vs null, client-side
  element validation
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Field: Gender
  Kind: enum
  Writable?: yes
  What it exercises: enum literal mapping, and a form asymmetry (below)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Field: AddressInfo
  Kind: Collection(Location)
  Writable?: technically
  What it exercises: a partial write silently corrupts the record
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Field: UserName, Concurrency
  Kind: key / computed
  Writable?: no
  What it exercises: silently ignored / server-managed

  Emails is the non-scalar you want. Measured behaviour:

  - PATCH replaces the whole collection, it does not merge or append. Clean,
  predictable semantics.
  - [] clears it (204). null returns 500. So "remove all emails" must serialize
  as an empty array, never null.
  - ["not-an-email"] is accepted with a 204. The server does no element
  validation at all, so the EmailAddress value object is doing real work rather
  than decorating a string.
  - A wrong element type ([123]) is a 500.

  Gender is a worthwhile second one, because of an asymmetry that is easy to get
  backwards:

  - In $filter, the enum must be fully qualified: Gender eq 
  Microsoft...PersonGender'Female' works, bare 'Female' is a 500.
  - In a PATCH body, it is exactly inverted: bare "Female" works, the qualified
  form is a 500.

  Unlike strings, the enum is validated ("Banana" and numeric 1 both 500). One
  catch: "Gender": null returns 204 but silently coerces to Male, since that is
  ordinal 0. Nullable in the metadata, not nullable in practice.

  AddressInfo: read-only is not merely acceptable, it is the right call

  A PATCH that omits the required City returns 204 No Content, then permanently
  corrupts the record. Every subsequent read fails:

  The property 'City[Nullable=False]' ... has a null value, which is not
  allowed.

  The blast radius is the whole entity set: one poisoned row makes GET People
  return 500, so the list screen dies too, not just that person's detail page.
  It is recoverable only by a blind PATCH (you cannot read the record to repair
  it), or ResetDataSource. A successful-looking write that bricks the collection
  is not something to put behind a console form.

  Three deltas to the architecture

  1. Sparse PATCH becomes a hard rule, not a preference. PersonMapper emits only
  fields the user actually edited. Sending the full entity would re-serialize
  AddressInfo on every save, turning a read-only field into a standing
  corruption risk. This also sidesteps the open-type behaviour (I confirmed a
  junk "Nonsense" sub-property inside Location persists happily).
  2. The ODataFilterTranslator / PersonMapper split is now load-bearing.
  Read-path and write-path enum literals are genuinely inverted, so a single
  shared "serialize a Gender" helper would be wrong on one side by construction.
  Two components, two golden-string tests.
  3. Domain models Gender as non-nullable with Unknown as the explicit third
  state, and the mapper never emits null for it. Likewise Emails clears as [].
  Both go in Infrastructure.Tests/QuirkHandling alongside a regression test
  asserting we never emit a partial Location.

  Net: the update feature exercises a scalar pair, a primitive collection with
  replace semantics and a null/empty trap, and a validated enum with
  direction-dependent encoding. That is a real surface, not just proving we can
  PATCH a string.

  Shall I scaffold now: solution and project files, docs/adr/ with ADR-001 to
  003, and a fourth ADR recording the writable-field decision and the
  AddressInfo rationale?

  Build and test status

  dotnet build   Build succeeded.  0 Warning(s)  0 Error(s)     (clean rebuild)
  dotnet test    Domain.Tests           2 passed,  5 skipped
                 Application.Tests      5 passed,  5 skipped
                 Infrastructure.Tests   1 passed, 18 skipped
                 Failed: 0

  The 28 skipped tests are named placeholders, one per behaviour we agreed needs
  covering, each carrying the reason in its skip message. The 8 passing ones
  are real: Result<T> semantics, the Gender ordinals (which matter because null
  coerces to ordinal 0), and a MockHttp smoke test proving the stub can produce
  the bodyless 204 the quirk tests depend on.

  Onion direction verified by dotnet list reference, not just by intent: Domain
  has zero project and zero package references; Application → Domain;
  Infrastructure → Application; Console → Application + Infrastructure.

  Decisions I made inside your brief

  FluentAssertions pinned to 7.2.2, not 8.10.0. Version 8.0.0 moved to a paid
  Xceed license for commercial use; 7.2.2 is the last Apache-2.0 release. Given
  this is a company account I pinned the free line rather than walk you into a
  license obligation. If you want a maintained 8.x-era API, AwesomeAssertions is
  an Apache-2.0 fork of the same surface and is a near drop-in. Flagged in a
  comment in Directory.Packages.props.

  xUnit v3 (3.2.2) rather than v2, as the current line for a greenfield .NET 10
  project. Verified it compiles and runs against FluentAssertions 7.2.2, since
  that pairing was the one real compatibility risk.

  RichardSzalay.MockHttp 7.0.0 for HttpMessageHandler stubbing, and NSubstitute 
  6.0.0 as you suggested (no change: it is the right pick, and it avoids Moq's
  SponsorLink history).

  Central Package Management via Directory.Packages.props. Versions are still
  explicit, just declared once.

  Classic .sln. The .NET 10 SDK now defaults to .slnx; I used --format sln for
  broader CI and IDE compatibility. Trivial to switch.

  Three analyzer rules I relaxed deliberately, each with the reason in
  .editorconfig: CA1000 (fires on the standard Result<T>.Success factory idiom),
  CA1515, CA1812. I also enabled GenerateDocumentationFile because IDE0005 will
  not run as a build diagnostic without it, and suppressed the CS1591 noise
  that comes with it.

  TreatWarningsAsErrors is on for src/ only, via a tests/Directory.Build.props
  that imports the root and overrides it. It found real issues during
  scaffolding rather than being decorative: a dead field in SessionUriProvider
  (removed, intent kept in the remarks) and two xUnit warnings about unthreaded
  cancellation tokens, which I fixed properly rather than suppressed given
  cancellation discipline is a theme here.

  On the ADRs

  docs/adr/ holds ADR-001 to ADR-004 plus a README index. The rationale text is
  the agreed wording carried over verbatim, not regenerated. ADR-003
  additionally records your OperationScope trigger, and OperationScope.cs itself
  carries that note in its remarks so it is visible at the point of change.

  Not done, by instruction

  No business logic. Every method is a signature with throw new 
  NotImplementedException(), and the two DependencyInjection classes register
  what exists but leave HTTP, cache and decorator wiring as commented intent.
  The interfaces and DTOs are real, since those are the architecture rather than
  the implementation.

  Ready to implement whenever you want to start, and the natural first slice is
  Infrastructure's session provider plus ODataStatusInterpreter, since
  everything else depends on those two behaving correctly.

  ("always bypassing any cache") is the contract the whole ETag design rests

  encoding inversion.

✻ Crunched for 2m 44s

> Implement Domain and Application per the approved design — use the exact      
  names already agreed (IPeopleRepository, PersonSummary, PagedResult,          
  PersonFilter, ConcurrencyToken), not new ones.                                
                                                                                
  Domain:                                                                       
  - Person aggregate with the value objects already scoped: UserName (key),     
  PersonName, EmailAddress (real validation, since the OData service does       
  none), Gender (non-nullable, Unknown as explicit third state), Address        
  (read-only per ADR-004).                                                      
  - ConcurrencyToken wrapping the ETag, required on any update path.            
  - DomainException for invariant violations only.                              
                                                                                
  Application:                                                                  
  - IPeopleRepository port: ListAsync, SearchAsync(PersonFilter,                
  CancellationToken), GetByIdAsync (cacheable), GetForUpdateAsync (never        
  cached, always fresh, returns the current ConcurrencyToken), UpdateAsync —    
  all with CancellationToken.                                                   
  - Result<T> for expected failures (NotFound, ValidationFailed, Conflict for a 
  stale ETag) — no OData-specific exception leaks into Console.                 
  - UpdatePerson command: sparse by construction — only carries fields the      
  caller actually set, plus the required ConcurrencyToken. Validation: required 
  scalar fields per the metadata, EmailAddress format (client-side, since the   
  server won't catch it), and enforce Emails clears as [] never null at the     
  mapping boundary (note this for Infrastructure to honor, even though the      
  mapping itself is Infrastructure's job).                                      
  - Validation approach: recommend FluentValidation vs manual guard clauses and 
  justify given this is 4 use cases, not 40.                                    
                                                                                
  Unit tests (xUnit + FluentAssertions, no mocking needed): all validation      
  rules including the Emails null/empty distinction and Gender                  
  ordinal/null-coercion behavior, plus Person/value-object invariants. Replace  
  the existing placeholder/skipped tests in Domain.Tests and Application.Tests  
  with real ones rather than adding alongside them.                             
                                                                                
  Show me the code, then run the tests and confirm they pass — including that   
  the 10 previously-skipped placeholders in these two projects are now real and 
  passing, not still skipped.                                                   

● Before writing, one design point I need to settle, because it changes the port
  signature.

  You've specified the command carries the ConcurrencyToken. That's correct
  optimistic concurrency: the token must come from the caller's read, so a 412
  means someone else genuinely changed the record. But the scaffolded
  UpdateAsync(Person, PersonChanges, ct) forces the handler to re-read to build
  a Person, which yields a fresh token and silently defeats conflict detection.
  So I'm replacing Person + PersonChanges with a single sparse PersonUpdate that
  carries the token. I'll flag this in the summary.

  Starting with Domain.

      79 +            return false;

      83 +        return true;

  Test results

  dotnet build: 0 warnings, 0 errors on a clean rebuild. All 10
  previously-skipped placeholders in these two projects are gone; both report
  Skipped: 0.

  Validation: hand-rolled guards, not FluentValidation

  Recommendation: manual, and it's not close at this size. UpdatePersonValidator
  is ~110 lines of static, dependency-free code.

  The decisive argument is that the rules already live in the Domain value 
  objects. EmailAddress.TryFrom is where email format is decided,
  PersonName.TryNormalizePart is where a required name part is decided.
  FluentValidation would give me a fluent way to restate those rules in a second
  place, or to wrap each one in a Must(...) lambda that adds indirection
  without adding a rule. The duplication risk is the thing I most wanted to
  avoid, and a rule DSL increases it.

  What the Application layer genuinely adds over the Domain is narrow: collect 
  messages instead of throwing on the first failure. That is the errors list and
  one loop. This is why each value object exposes both From (throws
  DomainException, for values the system controls) and TryFrom (reports, for
  user input). The split does the work FluentValidation would otherwise be hired
  for.

  Secondary reasons: it keeps Application's package list at one entry; and being
  static it's callable straight from a test with no container and no
  registration, which is why UpdatePersonValidatorTests has 30 tests and zero
  setup. Consistent with the MediatR call in ADR-003: no framework for 4 use
  cases. The revisit trigger is the same one, roughly 15 use cases or rules
  genuinely shared across many commands.

  Two signature changes I had to make

  UpdateAsync(Person, PersonChanges, ct) → UpdateAsync(PersonUpdate, ct).
  Flagged before writing, and it is the substantive change. You specified the
  command carries the ConcurrencyToken, which is right. But the old signature
  needed a whole Person, which the handler could only get by re-reading, and
  that read returns a fresh token, silently replacing the caller's. Every
  concurrent edit would then become an overwrite instead of a 412. PersonUpdate
  carries the caller's token directly, so the handler makes exactly one call.
  Three tests pin this: The_caller_token_is_passed_through_untouched,
  The_handler_does_not_re_read_the_person_first, and
  A_stale_token_is_reported_as_a_concurrency_conflict. PersonChanges became
  redundant and is deleted, since a null property already means "untouched".

  GetAsync → GetByIdAsync per your list, propagated to both Infrastructure
  placeholders.

  SearchAsync keeps page/pageSize alongside the filter, since it returns a
  PagedResult and your shorthand omitted them.

  The two behaviours you singled out

  Emails null vs empty is enforced in the type system and covered by four tests.
  null stays null (untouched), [] stays an empty list (clear), and
  The_two_empty_states_are_distinguishable_on_the_update asserts they never
  collapse into each other. The XML docs on PersonUpdate.Emails spell out the
  three-state contract Infrastructure must honour, including that [] must not
  serialise as null (a 500).

  Gender coercion is covered from both ends.
  Default_is_Male_which_is_exactly_what_a_null_write_coerces_to states the trap
  as an assertion, and A_null_gender_means_untouched_and_never_becomes_Male
  proves a null command field means "leave alone" rather than "write null".
  Unknown is a real member, so a nullable domain type would have promised a
  state the service cannot store.

  One thing worth knowing for Infrastructure

  EmailAddress uses System.Net.Mail.MailAddress with two checks layered on top,
  because MailAddress alone accepts both Foo <a@b.com> and a@b. Domain still has
  zero package references; MailAddress ships in the shared framework. I also
  removed NSubstitute from Application.Tests, as the hand-written
  FakePeopleRepository made it unused. It stays in Infrastructure.Tests.

✻ Brewed for 4m 37s

※ recap: We're building a TripPin OData console app in Onion architecture; Domain and Application are now implemented with 149 passing tests and a clean build. Next up is implementing Infrastructure, starting with the session URI provider and OData status interpreter. (disable recaps in /config)

> Implement the Infrastructure layer per the approved design use the exact      
  components already named, don't introduce new abstractions.                   
                                                                                
  ODataPeopleRepository (typed HttpClient via IHttpClientFactory, base address  
  from TripPinOptions):                                                         
  - ListAsync/SearchAsync via ODataQueryBuilder + ODataFilterTranslator: safe   
  $filter/$select/$top/$skip/$count construction, no string-concatenated user   
  input. PersonFilter never becomes a raw string outside these two components.  
  - GetByIdAsync: key-segment lookup, maps a 204 response to Result.NotFound    
  via ODataStatusInterpreter, never throws for that case.                       
  - GetForUpdateAsync: same as GetByIdAsync but explicitly bypasses any cache — 
  this is the fresh-ETag read the update path depends on.                       
  - UpdateAsync: sparse PATCH built by PersonMapper from PersonUpdate — only    
  fields actually set, AddressInfo never included, Emails serializes as []      
  never null, Gender serializes as the bare enum literal (not the               
  fully-qualified form used in $filter). Sends If-Match with the caller's       
  ConcurrencyToken from PersonUpdate — never re-reads a token. Maps 412 to      
  Result.Conflict, 428 to a logged error/fail-fast (this should be unreachable  
  by construction; treat it as a bug if it ever surfaces, not a transient fault 
  to retry).                                                                    
  - SessionUriProvider/SessionUriHandler: singleton, Lazy<Task<Uri>> resolution 
  shared by all concurrent callers, DelegatingHandler rewrites relative URIs    
  onto the resolved session base. Resilience handler outermost, session handler 
  inner, so a retried request re-resolves rather than caching a failed          
  resolution permanently.                                                       
                                                                                
  ResiliencePipelineSetup: Polly v8 (Microsoft.Extensions.Http.Resilience)      
  retry with exponential backoff + jitter on 5xx/408/429/network faults only,   
  honoring Retry-After; circuit breaker on the same set. Explicitly exclude 412 
  and 428 from both retry and breaker counting — these are semantic outcomes,   
  not transient faults.                                                         
                                                                                
  CachedPeopleRepository (decorator over ODataPeopleRepository, implements      
  IPeopleRepository, composed at the DI root — use cases and                    
  ODataPeopleRepository itself stay unaware of caching):                        
  - List/search cached ~60s, single-person GetByIdAsync ~120s, both as          
  Lazy<Task<T>> values to prevent cache stampede on concurrent misses of the    
  same key.                                                                     
  - GetForUpdateAsync always passes through, never cached.                      
  - On successful UpdateAsync, invalidate via PeopleCacheKeys' shared           
  CancellationChangeToken generation (evicts all list/search entries) and evict 
  the specific detail entry by key directly.                                    
                                                                                
  Structured logging via ILogger<T> at each boundary: request made, retry       
  attempted, cache hit/miss, update succeeded/conflicted. No PII beyond what's  
  already in the domain (email addresses are fine to log at debug, not info).   
                                                                                
  Tests fill the existing placeholder folders in Infrastructure.Tests, don't    
  add new ones:                                                                 
  - QueryBuilding: golden-string tests for $filter escaping and the             
  fully-qualified Gender enum form.                                             
  - QuirkHandling: stubbed HttpMessageHandler (RichardSzalay.MockHttp) tests    
  for the 204-not-found mapping, 412→Conflict, 428 fail-fast, session redirect  
  resolution, nextLink/paging, and — this one matters most — a sparse-PATCH     
  corruption regression test: send an update omitting AddressInfo, then assert  
  a subsequent GetByIdAsync on that person and a subsequent ListAsync both      
  still succeed rather than 500ing.                                             
  - Caching: hit/miss/invalidation behavior on CachedPeopleRepository,          
  including that concurrent misses on the same key don't double-invoke the      
  factory (stampede protection), and that GetForUpdateAsync never returns a     
  cached value.                                                                 
  - Integration (Trait-gated, excluded from the default fast run): a handful of 
  tests against the real TripPinServiceRW endpoint covering                     
  list/search/get/update happy paths. Each test that writes must include its    
  own teardown (ResetDataSource or equivalent) so a failure doesn't leave the   
  shared sandbox corrupted for the next run or for someone else re-running the  
  suite.                                                                        
                                                                                
  Run unit tests (excluding Integration) and show a clean pass, then run        
  Integration separately and show that too.

          +erson impossible

          +I, which is the

      136 +    /// </remarks>

          +)                                                             
      138 +    {                                                         
      139 +        if (dto.Emails is null || dto.Emails.Count == 0)      
      140 +        {                                                     
      141 +            return [];                                        
      142 +        }                                                     
      143 +                                                              
      144 +        var emails = new List<EmailAddress>(dto.Emails.Count);
      145 +                                                              
      146 +        foreach (var candidate in dto.Emails)                 
      147 +        {                                                     
      148 +            if (EmailAddress.TryFrom(candidate, out var email,
          + out var error))                                              
      149 +            {                                                 
      150 +                emails.Add(email);                            
      151 +            }                                                 
      152 +            else

          +ess for {UserName}: {Reason}",                                
      156 +                    dto.UserName,                             
      157 +                    error);                                   
      158 +            }                                                 
      159 +        }                                                     
      160 +                                                              
      161 +        return emails;                                        
      162 +    }

          +en cancellationToken)

          +oken).ConfigureAwait(false);                                  
       45 +        }

          +rationCanceledException)

       53 +    public void Invalidate()

          +ed; it will be resolved again.");

       63 +    private Lazy<Task<Uri>> Current()                         
       64 +    {                                                         
       65 +        lock (_gate)                                          
       66 +        {

       68 +                ResolveAsync,                                 
       69 +                LazyThreadSafetyMode.ExecutionAndPublication);
       70 +        }                                                     
       71 +    }                                                         
       72 +                                                              
       73 +    /// <summary>Clears the cache only if it still holds the f
          +ailed attempt.</summary>                                      
       74 +    private void Evict(Lazy<Task<Uri>> failed)                
       75 +    {                                                         
       76 +        lock (_gate)                                          
       77 +        {                                                     
       78 +            if (ReferenceEquals(_resolution, failed))         
       79 +            {                                                 
       80 +                _resolution = null;                           
       81 +            }                                                 
       82 +        }                                                     
       83 +    }

       85 +    private async Task<Uri> ResolveAsync()                    
       86 +    {                                                         
       87 +        var configured = EnsureTrailingSlash(_options.BaseAddr
          +ess);                                                         
       88 +                                                              
       89 +        _logger.LogDebug("Resolving the session base address f
          +rom {ConfiguredBase}.", configured);                          
       90 +                                                              
       91 +        var client = _httpClientFactory.CreateClient(ResolverC
          +lientName);                                                   
       92 +                                                              
       93 +        using var response = await client                     
       94 +            .GetAsync(configured, HttpCompletionOption.Respons
          +eHeadersRead, CancellationToken.None)                         
       95 +            .ConfigureAwait(false);                           
       96 +                                                              
       97 +        response.EnsureSuccessStatusCode();                   
       98 +                                                              
       99 +        // The service answers the bare root with a 302 to a s
          +ession-scoped                                                 
      100 +        // address, so the final request URI is the session ba
          +se. The resolver                                              
      101 +        // client follows redirects for exactly this reason.  
      102 +        var resolved = EnsureTrailingSlash(response.RequestMes
          +sage?.RequestUri ?? configured);                              
      103 +

          +to {SessionBase}.", resolved);                                
      105 +                                                              
      106 +        return resolved;                                      
      107 +    }                                                         
      108 +

          +n drop the last

      112 +    /// </summary>                                            
      113 +    private static Uri EnsureTrailingSlash(Uri uri) =>

       16  /// the breaker: a 412 means the ETag was stale and a replay s
           ends the same
       17  /// stale value, while a 428 means we failed to send If-Match 
           at all, which is
       18  /// a bug in our own code rather than a service fault.
       19 +/// <para>                                                    
       20 +/// Both strategies share one predicate, which is what makes t
          +he exclusion hold                                             
       21 +/// for the breaker too: Polly counts only the outcomes a stra
          +tegy handles, so                                              
       22 +/// an unhandled 412 registers as a success and cannot push th
          +e circuit open                                                
       23 +/// against a service that is behaving perfectly well.        
       24 +/// </para>                                                   
       25 +/// <para>                                                    
       26 +/// Retry is added before the breaker, making it the outer of 
          +the two, so a                                                 
       27 +/// burst of retries against a failing service still feeds the
          + breaker.                                                     
       28 +/// </para>                                                   
       29  /// </remarks>
       30  public static class ResiliencePipelineSetup
       31  {
       32 +    public const string PipelineName = "TripPin.OData";       
       33 +                                                              
       34      public static IHttpClientBuilder AddTripPinResilience(
       35          this IHttpClientBuilder builder,
       20 -        ResilienceOptions options) => throw new NotImplemented
          -Exception();                                                  
       36 +        ResilienceOptions options)                            
       37 +    {                                                         
       38 +        ArgumentNullException.ThrowIfNull(builder);           
       39 +        ArgumentNullException.ThrowIfNull(options);           
       40 +                                                              
       41 +        builder.AddResilienceHandler(PipelineName, (pipeline, 
          +context) =>                                                   
       42 +        {                                                     
       43 +            var logger = context.ServiceProvider              
       44 +                .GetRequiredService<ILoggerFactory>()         
       45 +                .CreateLogger(PipelineName);                  
       46 +                                                              
       47 +            pipeline.AddRetry(new HttpRetryStrategyOptions    
       48 +            {                                                 
       49 +                MaxRetryAttempts = options.MaxRetryAttempts,  
       50 +                BackoffType = DelayBackoffType.Exponential,   
       51 +                UseJitter = true,                             
       52 +                Delay = TimeSpan.FromSeconds(options.BaseDelay
          +Seconds),                                                     
       53 +                ShouldRetryAfterHeader = true,                
       54 +                ShouldHandle = arguments =>                   
       55 +                    ValueTask.FromResult(ShouldTreatAsTransien
          +t(arguments.Outcome)),                                        
       56 +                OnRetry = arguments =>                        
       57 +                {                                             
       58 +                    logger.LogWarning(                        
       59 +                        "Retrying OData request, attempt {Atte
          +mptNumber} after {RetryDelay}. Outcome: {Outcome}.",          
       60 +                        arguments.AttemptNumber + 1,          
       61 +                        arguments.RetryDelay,                 
       62 +                        Describe(arguments.Outcome));         
       63 +                                                              
       64 +                    return default;                           
       65 +                },                                            
       66 +            });                                               
       67 +                                                              
       68 +            pipeline.AddCircuitBreaker(new HttpCircuitBreakerS
          +trategyOptions                                                
       69 +            {                                                 
       70 +                FailureRatio = 0.5,                           
       71 +                MinimumThroughput = options.CircuitBreakerMini
          +mumThroughput,                                                
       72 +                SamplingDuration = TimeSpan.FromSeconds(30),  
       73 +                BreakDuration = TimeSpan.FromSeconds(options.C
          +ircuitBreakerDurationSeconds),

       75 +                    ValueTask.FromResult(ShouldTreatAsTransien
          +t(arguments.Outcome)),

       77 +                {                                             
       78 +                    logger.LogError(                          
       79 +                        "OData circuit opened for {BreakDurati
          +on}.",                                                        
       80 +                        arguments.BreakDuration);             
       81 +                                                              
       82 +                    return default;                           
       83 +                },                                            
       84 +                OnClosed = _ =>                               
       85 +                {                                             
       86 +                    logger.LogInformation("OData circuit close
          +d.");                                                         
       87 +                                                              
       88 +                    return default;                           
       89 +                },                                            
       90 +            });                                               
       91 +        });                                                   
       92 +                                                              
       93 +        return builder;                                       
       94 +    }                                                         
       95 +                                                              
       96 +    private static bool ShouldTreatAsTransient(Outcome<HttpRes
          +ponseMessage> outcome)                                        
       97 +    {                                                         
       98 +        if (outcome.Exception is not null)                    
       99 +        {                                                     
      100 +            return outcome.Exception is HttpRequestException o
          +r TimeoutException;                                           
      101 +        }                                                     
      102 +                                                              
      103 +        return outcome.Result is not null                     
      104 +            && ODataStatusInterpreter.IsTransient(outcome.Resu
          +lt.StatusCode);                                               
      105 +    }                                                         
      106 +                                                              
      107 +    private static string Describe(Outcome<HttpResponseMessage
          +> outcome) =>
