  On that second point, so it isn't lost — three things I decided not to change,
  and want to state as tradeoffs rather than silently leave:

  - System.Net.Mail.MailAddress in Domain. The namespace reads like I/O, which
  is momentarily alarming in a dependency-free layer. It's a pure parser with no
  network behaviour and no package reference, and the alternative (hand-rolled
  email regex) is materially worse. Keeping, documented.
  - PersonEditScreen injects IPeopleRepository directly for the fresh pre-edit
  read. It's an Application-owned port so the dependency direction holds, but
  it's the one place Console touches a port instead of a use case.
  - TripPinOptions.BaseAddress carries both [Required] and a non-null default,
  so the [Required] can never actually fire, and the URL is duplicated with
  appsettings.json.

  Review findings

  Onion rules: clean. Domain has zero project and package references. No
  Infrastructure, HTTP, Polly, JSON or caching types appear in Domain or
  Application, checked by namespace and by bare type name.

  Nullable: 0 warnings, 0 suppressions. No #pragma, no SuppressMessage, no
  <NoWarn> beyond CS1591. I went further than the check asked: there were 13 
  null-forgiving ! operators, which are suppression by another name. Adding
  [MemberNotNullWhen(true, nameof(Value))] to Result<T>.IsSuccess and
  [NotNullWhen(false)] to the Blank helpers eliminated all 13 and restored real
  compiler checking at those sites.

  Secrets/URLs: clean. No secrets anywhere (the only grep hits were build
  artifacts naming the UserSecrets package). The service URL appears only in
  appsettings.json and as a default in TripPinOptions.

  Comments: removed one restating summary, and corrected one that had gone
  factually stale after the timeout fix (IsTransient's doc claimed it covered
  "transport errors" when it only takes a status code). Everything else explains
  non-obvious why, rooted in measured behaviour.

  The bug I found by testing my own README

  Writing the README made me run the command I was documenting, and it exposed a
  real defect: dotnet run --project src/TripPin.Console from the repo root
  silently ignored appsettings.json entirely. Host.CreateApplicationBuilder
  roots configuration at the current directory, not the binary. The app fell
  back to TripPinOptions defaults with nothing on screen to say so; cache TTLs,
  timeouts, page size and log levels were all silently wrong. It was only
  visible because lifetime logs appeared at INF despite config setting them to
  Warning.

  Fixed by rooting content at AppContext.BaseDirectory. Verified: those log
  lines are now gone.

  Worth noting why this hid so well. The hardcoded default on BaseAddress meant
  a total config failure still produced a working app pointing at the right
  service. That is the tradeoff below.

  Flagged, kept as documented tradeoffs

  - System.Net.Mail.MailAddress in Domain. The namespace reads like I/O in a
  dependency-free layer. It is a pure parser, framework-provided, no package
  reference, and the alternative (hand-rolled email regex) is materially worse.
  - TripPinOptions.BaseAddress has both [Required] and a non-null default, so
  [Required] can never fire, and the URL is duplicated with appsettings.json.
  Kept because the app works standalone, and the startup banner prints the
  address in use, so a fallback is at least visible.
  - PersonEditScreen injects IPeopleRepository directly for the fresh pre-edit
  read. Dependency direction holds (it is an Application-owned port), but it is
  the one place Console touches a port rather than a use case.
  - Result<T> with a value-type T returns default rather than null on failure.
  No such usage in src/, only in tests.

  README

  README.md covers local run, architecture with links to the four ADRs, unit vs
  integration test commands, and limitations: no auth (nothing to authenticate
  against), no persistence beyond the service, session-scoped data, the
  two-round-trip ETag flow, and the three headline quirks (count-after-paging,
  the Gender encoding inversion, the AddressInfo corruption risk).

  Containerization is documented as deliberately skipped for an interactive
  single-user tool with no deployment target, with a sentence on the shape it
  would take if deployed, including that SessionUriProvider is the first thing
  to reconsider since per-process session affinity does not survive horizontal
  scaling.

  The AI Engineering Notes section is brief and honest: it names the three
  substantive corrections (the UpdateAsync signature that would have silently
  clobbered the caller's ETag, the @odata.count quirk that only live integration
  testing caught, and the untested resilience pipeline that was hiding the
  timeout and MaxRetryAttempts: 0 bugs), and points to the exported transcript
  as the complete record rather than pretending to be one.

  The Dependencies warning: what I found

  It is not a package problem. I ruled that out with evidence rather than
  assumption:

  ┌───────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │    Suspected cause    │                     Finding                     │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ NuGet restore warning │ dotnet restore --force -v normal → 0 warnings,  │
  │                       │ no NU**** codes                                 │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Package vulnerability │ --vulnerable --include-transitive → none, all 7 │
  │  advisory             │  projects                                       │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Deprecated package    │ --deprecated → none, all 7 projects             │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Missing version under │ All 11 versions resolve; no NU1008/NU1604       │
  │  CPM                  │                                                 │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Restore diagnostics   │ No logs array in any project.assets.json —      │
  │                       │ NuGet recorded zero diagnostics                 │
  └───────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  That last one is the decisive check: the assets file is exactly what Solution
  Explorer reads to decorate those nodes, and it contains no diagnostics at all.
  So the CLI-side graph is clean, and the icon is coming from IDE-side
  resolution.

  I can't see your Solution Explorer, so I won't pretend to have observed the
  icon itself. But the investigation surfaced two genuine root causes that both
  produce exactly this symptom, and both are real defects worth fixing
  regardless:

  1. No global.json, with four SDKs installed (2.2.207, 8.0.405, 9.0.316,
  10.0.302). SDK selection was implicit "latest wins," and Visual Studio
  resolves its SDK independently of the CLI. If the IDE picks an SDK that can't
  resolve net10.0, the design-time build fails and every Dependencies sub-node
  (Analyzers, Frameworks, Packages, Projects) shows a warning, which matches the
  symptom precisely. Now pinned to 10.0.302 with rollForward: latestFeature. If
  .NET 10 is genuinely absent from the IDE, you now get an explicit "SDK not
  found" instead of mystery triangles, which is the right failure mode.

  2. Two package sources under Central Package Management. Your machine
  registers nuget.org plus Microsoft Visual Studio Offline Packages. Multiple
  unmapped sources with CPM is precisely what NuGet raises NU1507 for, and it
  makes restore machine-dependent. Added nuget.config with <clear /> and package
  source mapping pinning everything to nuget.org. Verified: dotnet nuget list 
  source now shows one source and a forced restore stays at 0 warnings.

  Neither is a suppression. Both make the build deterministic instead of
  dependent on machine state.

  If the icon persists after a reload, the next thing I'd want is the IDE's own
  signal: the Error List filtered to Build + IntelliSense, or View → Output → 
  Solution after a restore. That distinguishes an SDK-resolution failure from
  something else, and I can act on it directly.

  BaseAddress: resolved

  Picked remove [Required], keep the default, document why. Two reasons: the app
  targets one fixed public sample service, so a built-in address is the correct
  value rather than a placeholder; and [Required] on a property that always
  holds a value is dead metadata that reads as enforcement while enforcing
  nothing. The eleven [Range] attributes remain, so ValidateDataAnnotations
  still does real work.

  The comment states the risk a default carries (masking a config file that
  never loaded) and where that is actually addressed: the content-root fix, plus
  the startup banner printing the address in use so a fallback is visible
  rather than silent.

  README
